#7394. Political Campaigns Spent a Near-Record $331 Per Voter, But Waste Forces Rethink

Where are the most expensive media markets in the U.S.? At least for political campaigns in 2026, they were Maine, Alaska, and Iowa.

These thinly populated battleground states saw spending of up to $331 per registered voter for the 2026 election cycle, according to data from AdImpact and state voter rolls.

That came as total political spending hit a record $11.9 billion across TV, digital, and audio, exceeding the 2024 presidential year’s $11.2 billion. None of that includes substantial direct mail or outdoor campaigns.

Maine saw the highest spending per voter at $331, followed by Alaska at $313 and Iowa at $252. They fell just shy of a record set by Montana in 2024, which got $335 per voter thanks to a competitive Senate seat. Competitive Senate and gubernatorial races fueled a record number of three-digit outlays per voter in 2026, including Ohio ($112) and Kansas ($102).

Spending per voter doubled and even tripled from two years ago in all five triple-digit-spending states. Republicans outspent Democrats in four of those five but lost key races in all of them, per AdImpact, with the Alaska Senate race still being counted.

That so much money failed to change outcomes could force a major rethink of 2028 strategy.

Heavy spending meant local ads spilling into national buys. By October, Senate campaign ads from Iowa, Texas, Florida, Maine, and Kansas ran on some national broadcasts of NCAA and NFL football games.

A shift to digital and connected TV spending this year made targeting theoretically easier, but didn’t guarantee reaching the right people.

Sal Tuzzeo, a Fabric Media executive based near Cleveland, said his 8-year-old son was bombarded by political ads, even though he was watching age-appropriate content on the big screen from YouTube creators TerraGreen, The Fish Guys, and Unspeakable.

“The amount of political money flowing into streaming and CTV seemed absurd,” Tuzzeo said. “The mistargeting was egregious.” He attributed it to “a broad reach land grab coupled with overt aversion to frequency capping.”

Over the past few months, nearly every ad in every pod his son watched was political, Tuzzeo said. His child could recite verbatim ads dealing with abortion, sex changes, crack cocaine, and domestic violence.

Republicans outspent Democrats by a $5.5 billion to $5 billion margin nationwide, per AdImpact. But that advantage made little difference. For example, of 87 candidates backed by Elon Musk’s America PAC, 80 lost, according to Travis Trawick, CEO of nonpartisan election tech firm FullPAC.

Too much money went into broadcast over the final five weeks, when time is most expensive and when “at least in this election, most people’s minds were already made up,” Trawick said. “Running the same ad in front of people 60 times just has diminishing returns.”

For 2028, he believes campaigns will shift spending earlier in the cycle and toward AI-driven digital and social strategies, using AI not just to optimize media but also to collect return path signals and shape creative that spawns voter engagement.

“Being able to create a conversation between a voter and a candidate or an issue, not just talking at them, and doing that at scale, is something that’s never really been possible before,” Trawick said. “Now it is.”

It runs on hundreds of thousands of shared, VPS and dedicated hosting servers, under cPanel, Plesk, DirectAdmin and standalone.

You'll drive technical decisions, improve architecture, establish engineering standards, and help teams build sustainable solutions.

#7386. Amazon is Letting Customers Text Its Shopping Assistant

Amazon is giving customers the ability to text its AI shopping assistant, and serving up ads along the way.

Some customers have the ability to text requests to Amazon’s Alexa for Shopping agent, which finds products, builds shopping carts, makes purchases, and provides delivery updates.

The texting feature is initially available to a small group of U.S. customers by invitation only, and those customers will receive a text message inviting them to try it. Amazon said it will expand the option to more shoppers in the coming weeks.

“Customers often jot down what they need the moment they think of it in their notes, in their messages, in their group chats,” said Rajiv Mehta, vp of conversational shopping at Amazon, in a statement.

Mitchell Parton is ADWEEK's senior commerce reporter, covering retail media as well as the rise of agentic and social commerce. Before joining ADWEEK in 2026, he was a reporter at Digiday Media's Modern Retail and before that wrote for several regional publications in Texas.

#7372. Senior Infrastructure Engineer II at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

We're looking for a Senior Infrastructure Engineer II to take ownership of a large share of Kit's core infrastructure: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, platform tooling, and day-to-day support. You'll also build the paved paths that let engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents test and ship changes safely.

The infrastructure supports creators who depend on Kit to reach their audiences and earn a living. When it's healthy, nobody notices. That's the job.

This is a full-time Individual Contributor role for an experienced infrastructure engineer who likes owning production systems end to end, takes on-call seriously, leans into automation and enjoys making other people's work easier.

You'll report to Mojtaba Hosseini, VP of Engineering at Kit. Day to day, you'll work most closely with Kris Hamoud and Ivan Mora Perez.

You'll also partner with product engineers across Kit, and with teams outside engineering who rely on infrastructure to get their work done. Once you're ramped up, you'll join a three-person on-call rotation, which works out to roughly one week in three.

First Week: Get access to our AWS accounts, Kubernetes clusters, monitoring, and CI/CD pipelines. Read through our runbooks and architecture docs. Take part in Get To Know Yous (GTKYs) with Kris, Ivan, and the engineering teams you'll support.

First Month: Pick up day-to-day infrastructure support requests and start working through them with Kris and Ivan as backup. Ship your first improvement, such as a version upgrade or a fix to a gap in monitoring. Shadow on-call shifts. Write down what you learn along the way, especially the parts that only lived in someone's head or those that can be automated or improved.

First Six Months: Own core infrastructure areas end to end: databases, Kubernetes, version upgrades, and platform tooling. Take your full share of on-call, which you'll start by the end of your first quarter. Become the go-to person for day-to-day infrastructure questions. Find one recurring source of pages and make it go away. Ship a first paved path, such as a sandbox environment where AI agents or non-engineers can safely build and test changes.

First Year: You're the primary owner of a large part of Kit's core infrastructure, and you lead projects in it without needing direction. Uptime stays at 99.9% or better, page volume is down, and you've found real cost savings. Your paved paths are in regular use by engineers, non-engineers, and AI agents. You've built observability good enough for AI agents to handle some on-call load. You've documented your decisions so others can build on your work.

Running production infrastructure on AWS at scale, including managed relational databases, networking, and load balancing

Operating Kubernetes in production: upgrades, capacity planning, and troubleshooting

Managing infrastructure as code and maintaining CI/CD pipelines that engineers trust

Building monitoring and alerting that catches real problems early and doesn't page people for noise

Diagnosing complex, multi-service incidents calmly, then fixing the underlying cause

Cutting cloud costs through right-sizing and reserved capacity without hurting reliability

Writing clear runbooks, docs, and decision write-ups

Nice to have: experience with Cloudflare, ephemeral or sandbox environments, or email sending infrastructure

Around 7 or more years in infrastructure, platform, DevOps, or SRE roles

Owning production infrastructure for a product where downtime costs customers money

Carrying a pager for systems you owned, and making that on-call rotation quieter over time

Leading a major upgrade or migration, such as a database or Kubernetes version upgrade, with little or no customer impact

Building self-service tooling or paved paths that other engineers actually adopted

Working on a small infrastructure team where you couldn't hand problems off to a specialist

You own what you run. You spot infrastructure debt and fix it without waiting to be asked, and you follow through until it's done.

You build quality into the work. Upgrades have rollback plans, maintenance windows are planned around customers, and "good enough" is a decision, not a default.

You leverage AI effectively. AI helps you increase quality and velocity where it matters most. You know when to delegate and when to dive in, while holding accountability.

You make work easier for everyone else. You'd rather build a tool or write a guide once than answer the same question ten times.

You teach what you know. You write things down so the next person, or the next AI agent, can build on your work.

You work in public. You share context and tradeoffs openly, including during incidents and when things go wrong.

You use AI thoughtfully. It's part of how you work, and you help others adopt it without creating new risks.

You remember who's on the other end. An outage means a creator's launch email doesn't go out, and that shapes how you prioritize.

Wanting a well-defined backlog handed to you each week. Here, you'll find much of the work yourself and set priorities with Kris.

Preferring not to be on call. On-call is a core part of this role, shared across a team of three.

You are skeptical of AI’s capabilities, or conversely use AI without good judgement and accountability.

Wanting mostly greenfield architecture. A lot of this work is running, upgrading, and improving systems that already exist.

Needing layers of approval before acting.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time—so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

This role is part of a three-person on-call rotation, so during your on-call week you'll need to be able to respond to pages outside your usual working hours.

We believe PTO should always take priority over on-call. We schedule on-call around people's lives, not the other way around. On-call is a shared responsibility, but taking time off should never be a challenge

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we're hiring at our level 4 ($197,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

We cover up to $2,100 per month toward medical premiums, with dental and vision premiums fully covered. We offer Health Insurance plans through Aetna

$2,000 equipment allowance for your first two years, $1,000 budget every following two years. Company-provided laptops are issued to every Kit team member and are not included in the equipment budget

Individual learning + development budget ($3,500/year)

Creator meetup budget ($50/month) to connect with creators over coffee, lunch, or other in-person meetups

Childcare benefit up to $3,000 annually through JOON

Twenty (20) days of paid time off during each year of employment

Two weeks of paid sick time each year, including mental health + well being days

Twelve (12) weeks paid parental leave and flexible scheduling in your child’s first year

Up to six weeks of paid bereavement leave, medical leave, and disaster after six months of employment, two weeks of each paid leave in your first six months

Winter Break Closure: Kit closes for a week at the end of December, giving everyone a collective break to enjoy the holiday season. Essential support services remain available, with teams coordinating to ensure coverage during this period

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

Fantastic in-person or virtual retreats with the team twice a year

#7364. Brandweek 2026: The ‘Why’ Test Behind Mars’ Boldest Ideas ft. Rankin Carroll

The best creative risks should make you a little nervous. But they should never make you ask why your brand is taking them.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Rankin Carroll, chief brand officer of Mars Snacking, to unpack how brands like M&M’s, Snickers and Skittles stay culturally relevant without treating boldness as an excuse for chaos.

Rankin takes us behind the scenes of the 2023 Snickers AI experiment that felt “potentially crazy” at the time.

He also gets candid about Mars’ creator learning curve and the responsibility marketers have to the next generation.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Rankin Carroll is Chief Brand Officer of Mars Snacking, where he works across some of the company’s most recognizable brands, including M&M’s, Snickers and Skittles. He has spent more than 20 years at Mars across marketing, advertising, general management and global and regional leadership roles. His career has taken him from Toronto to the UK and at one point, included running an ice cream business along the way.

Today, Rankin focuses on what he describes as the stewardship of distinctive, iconic brands: protect what makes them valuable, make them stronger than when you received them, and still create enough tension to keep them culturally relevant.

[04:33] The Snickers AI Bet That Looked “Potentially Crazy” — In 2023, Mars put Snickers into an agentic creative experience at a point when the safety and reputational risks looked far less understood than they do today. The project involved roughly ten tech partners, legal and security leadership, and a list of 8,000 blocked words. The takeaway is much bigger than “take more risk.” It’s that ambitious experimentation becomes possible when the organization commits to solving the risk together.

[07:56] Earn the Right to Take Risks — Rankin draws a hard line between calculated cultural risk and performative brand behavior. Skittles could maintain its relationship with the LGBTQ+ community during a more volatile period because the brand had spent years building credibility there. His filter for every big swing is simple: Why us? Why this brand? If there is no strategically convincing answer, the idea does not deserve the risk.

[13:42] Are We Preparing the Next Generation for What Marketing Becomes Next? — Rankin raises one of the more uncomfortable questions from Brandweek 2026: what happens to junior marketers when the profession changes around AI? He expects the practice to look completely different within three years and openly says he does not yet have a satisfying answer for how marketers entering the industry should be trained. For him, that makes talent development a leadership responsibility, not an HR afterthought.

[15:03] “A Thousand Pilots and No Progress” — Rankin believes leaving AI adoption to disconnected enthusiasts across an organization is a recipe for chaos. CMOs need to own the agenda alongside technology leadership, bringing top-down clarity to investment, priorities and what the company should build, buy, rent or borrow. His warning is simple: just because you can use AI somewhere does not mean you should.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

This award recognizes the top CMOs whose decisions directly impact business growth, backed with data from Brand Finance

Plus, why doing the right thing beats the short-term number.

#7358. Meerkat’s Dustin Black Makes an Art of Staying Curious

Creative energy doesn’t ebb when the world stops. So when the pandemic stalled agency work in 2020, Dustin Black turned his lifelong pursuit of creativity on his home.

The career art director—recently named chief creative officer and partner at full-service Minneapolis creative agency Meerkat (formerly Preston Spire)—has twin daughters, who were 6 years old at the time. He’d been planning to turn their family mantra—“Be brave, be curious, be kind”—into a giant banner for their home, and suddenly had the time to make it.

Using felt sourced from New York-based manufacturer Oxford Pennant, Black’s banner infused some much-needed cheer into their basement. He also shared the finished product with the company.

“Oxford Pennant just absolutely loved it,” he said, so much so that they began producing a pennant using Black’s design—and offered him a supply of felt for future projects.

In fact, Black did have another idea in the works: He’d been sculpting a 5-foot-long fish out of foam that was in need of scales. As it happened, at-home cutting machine maker Cricut had also taken an interest in Black’s banner and offered to sponsor his work.

All that was left was for Black to take every hands-on art lesson, from fingerpainting as a toddler to his double major in art at the University of Nebraska-Lincoln, and apply it to a new kind of blank slate. “I had to figure out, what would a felt fish look like? I started playing, and I suddenly had my first fish covered in felt scales,” he said.

Black has been chasing new creative highs ever since, from making ever-more complex scales to cladding his fish in felt flowers instead, then turning to sculpting birds. Each sculpture begins with elaborate sketches, then Black figures out the size and frames it out in metal, wood, and foam.

The Cricut does the tedious work of cutting out the felt, then Black glues on each piece and further decorates it with paint and other materials. Everything is done inside his 25-square-foot home office on nights and weekends.

Since the beginning, he’s involved his girls to “show them it’s OK to try something new and fail” (they now have their own art projects as well). Black also documents the creative process of each piece on Instagram, including his mistakes and how he moved on from them.

“I’m trying to put out there the notion of we’re all beginners, and we’re all figuring it out, and it might look good in the end but it took a lot to get there,” he explained.

His sculptures have been exhibited in galleries, at events and, most recently, for a year at Minneapolis-Saint Paul International Airport. Having them in a public space “has been a lot of fun because I get random texts from people: ‘Look at what my family group chat is talking about!’” he said.

The pursuit of new shapes, styles, and designs is what drives Black, which is why he’s resisted taking commissions that would dictate what he makes—or worse, copy his previous work.

His latest project, an eagle with feathers made of polymer clay that Black hand-painted, has pride of place inside the Meerkat offices. With the widespread suspicion of AI in creative work, Black knows the value in clients being able to see and touch a piece of art made in-house.

He also hopes it inspires the kind of creativity that drives good work: “We need to be doing new things. We need to be solving complex problems. And we need to use curiosity to approach it.”

Off the Clock chronicles the hobbies, sports, and side hustles of people in the advertising and marketing world. We always welcome your nominations at eva.kis@adweek.com.

Eva Kis is the production editor at Adweek.

#7353. Fox Corp. Completes $22 Billion Deal for Roku

There is a new sheriff in Roku City: Fox has completed its purchase of the streaming and tech company.

The $22 billion deal closed today, combining Fox Corp.’s broadcast, news, sports, streaming, and local news portfolio with Roku’s open connected TV platform and first-party data from more than 100 million global streaming households.

The merged company becomes the third-largest entity in U.S. television by share of viewing. Fox says the addition of Roku will create new opportunities to serve its viewers, advertisers, and partners.

Executive chair and CEO of Fox Corp. Lachlan Murdoch said acquiring Roku advances the media company’s long-term strategy and will continue to “relentlessly serve audiences and partners, while reinforcing the openness and choice that is the essence of the Roku platform.”

Roku will be folded into Fox Digital Platforms, which also houses fellow FAST service Tubi, Fox One, and Red Seat Ventures. The streaming platform’s founder, Anthony Wood, will join Fox’s board of directors and serve as co-chair of Fox Digital Platforms alongside Paul Cheesbrough, the unit’s CEO.

When the merger was announced in June, the companies said they were looking to create a “scaled next-generation media and technology company” to prosper in today’s environment of increasing consumption of live sports, news, and streaming.

Roku stockholders will receive $95.30 in cash and 0.9799 shares of Fox Class A common stock for each share of Roku Class A and Roku Class B common stock they owned before the deal closed, under the merger agreement.

Separately, Fox shareholders will now own approximately 74% of the outstanding shares of Fox common stock, and the remaining approximately 26% will go to former Roku shareholders.

Reflecting on Roku’s acquisition, Woods said in a statement that he was proud of what his team has built and that combining with Fox provides an opportunity to accelerate its vision, scale faster, and be more aggressive in innovation for its respective audiences.

Woods added, “I couldn’t be more excited about what we’ll accomplish together.”

#7339. #AskNewser: CNN, NBC News and NewsNation Executives Reflect on Their Election Night Coverage

As the dust settles from a historic midterm election that saw the Democratic Party surf a blue wave to capture both the Senate and the House, news outlets are downshifting from special coverage and settling back into regular programming.

With 31 million viewers tuning in Tuesday night across 13 networks, TVNewser recently spoke to NBC News’ evp, creative, innovation & field production Marc Greenstein, whose network was the No.1 network during primetime in total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo, as well as CNN’s svp, Washington & special events programming Abigail Crutchfield, whose network was the most-watched cable news network in the prized demo, and NewsNation’s political director Chris Stirewalt, whose network in coordination with Decision Desk HQ was the first one to call both the House and the Senate for the Democrats, for a post-election AskNewser on what worked well during their live election coverage.

TVNewser: What worked well from a coverage and technology standpoint on election night?

Greenstein: Steve Kornacki continues to be the best at breaking down the vote, providing context and helping viewers understand not just what is happening, but where the night is headed.

We combined editorial and technology in a new way, allowing Steve to contribute to the linear broadcast while simultaneously hosting his own dedicated streaming feed from the same studio as Savannah Guthrie and Tom Llamas. This allowed us to produce two distinct broadcasts just feet apart, each tailored to its audience, without losing the editorial connection between our talent.

Extensive planning for multiple election scenarios allowed us to react quickly as race calls came in. We deployed multiple correspondents across nearly every battleground state, covering both voting developments and candidate headquarters. This gave us a strong presence in the places driving the story and allowed us to bring firsthand reporting and reaction into the broadcast throughout the night.

We played more than 35,000 graphics across our zones and platforms, delivering results and analysis with speed and clarity. We brought together broadcast, streaming and digital coverage under a consistent editorial and visual approach, while tailoring the experience to each platform. The launch of our dedicated vertical video feed was another important step in reaching audiences in new ways without compromising the depth or quality of our journalism.

Cruthchfield: It was having all of our experts and talent together in one place. It gave the DC bureau great energy, and it was fun to watch the reporters and anchors work together to unpack the results.

Our director, Reza Baktar, designed a phenomenal set that gave us more mobility than ever and made it easy to get to any data set we needed at any moment. I also loved giving John King the ability to analyze the data at the magic wall or to bring his mini magic wall over to our analyst tables to be a part of the conversation. And I loved King’s new vertical magic wall, which opened up new storytelling options.

Stirewalt: You can’t beat the cards. We had such an advantage by having Decision Desk HQ (DDHQ) on our side. I think we did a good job of bringing viewers into the process and explaining what we were doing, but when you get down to it, the advantage of being so far ahead of the competition was just amazing.

TN: What was the biggest surprise of the night?

Greenstein: The biggest surprise was how quickly we were able to call control of the Senate. While there was a general sense that it could be a good night for Democrats, I think the early call caught many of us a little off guard. That said, we had planned for a wide range of scenarios and were prepared to stay on air throughout the night across the broadcast network, NBC News Now and Kornacki Cam.

Another pleasant surprise was how well the AI audio technology worked, allowing us to produce the linear and streaming broadcasts simultaneously from the same studio. We had limited time to rehearse with the technology, so there were certainly some nerves heading into the night. But it performed remarkably well and allowed us to pull off something we had never done before.

Cruthchfield: I think perhaps how quickly we were able to project the major results of the night. We always plan for every scenario, but this Tuesday we got answers from the voters quickly.

Stirewalt: How much work I wasted preparing for close elections in so many places. I was buckled in for tight contests in battleground states. We found out by 8:30 p.m. ET or so that what we really needed to get good at was going deep on places like Mississippi.

TN: Share the tricks you used to keep yourself awake and alert on Tuesday night.

Greenstein: Coffee, Diet Coke, and the adrenaline of being in the director’s chair! In my role, I rarely get the opportunity to direct. The last time was Election Night 2024. So getting back in the chair and working alongside the most talented people in the business, both on air and behind the scenes, pushes me to be at the top of my game. And I love every minute of it.

Cruthchfield: A lot of hot tea, a phenomenal team, and a big bag of gummy bears.

Stirewalt: I don’t need any outside stimulants. When the results started pouring in, I was so thrilled at how DDHQ and our team were doing that I was just high on life.

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. CloudLinux Inc. develops the Imunify family of security products, designed to protect hosting environments from a wide range of cyber threats. One of these solutions is Imunify Email, an email security product that protects hosting servers against spam, phishing, and other email-based threats.

Imunify Email filters mail on about 5,000 hosting servers. Today the filter is rspamd with rules and a scoring model we train in-house. We are replacing it with a transformer we trained ourselves that runs on the customer's own CPU in under 100 MB and already beats rspamd at every operating point we measured. We are looking for a Senior Machine Learning Engineer to join our team and work alongside the engineer currently responsible for both the existing spam-filtering pipeline and the new ML model. Together, you will drive the development, improvement, and production rollout of our email security technology.

#7302. Software Engineer - Storage at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

#7290. Inside the Supreme Court case that could decide Title IX employee protections

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

This month, the high court will hear arguments on whether higher education workers have the right to sue their institutions for sex-based discrimination.

A critical question facing the U.S. Supreme Court in its 2026-27 term is whether employees of federally funded educational institutions can sue their employers for sex discrimination under Title IX.

On Nov. 30, the justices will hear arguments in Crowther v. Board of Regents of the University System of Georgia, a case that experts agree will have big implications on future litigation brought by employees against colleges and universities.

Georgia Institute of Technology, more commonly known as Georgia Tech, fired its head women’s basketball coach MaChelle Joseph in 2019, less than two months after she filed an internal complaint alleging that the university was discriminating against her and her sports program by not providing the same resources that it did for the men’s basketball team.

The university countered that it terminated Joseph following an independent investigation that revealed certain players felt bullied by her.

In 2020, Augusta University — also part of the University System of Georgia — reassigned and later decided against renewing the contract of fine arts professor Thomas Crowther after a Title IX investigation into an anonymous sexual harassment complaint. Crowther claimed that the university ignored numerous witness statements in his favor.

Both Crowther and Joseph sued the system’s regent board alleging sex discrimination under Title IX, which prohibits sex-based discrimination in federally funded education programs. The Atlanta-based 11th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals consolidated the cases and dismissed both Title IX claims, finding the 1972 law did not provide individuals with the right to sue for sex discrimination in employment.

Most other federal appellate courts disagree, with eight circuit courts having ruled that Title IX permits employees to sue the institutions that employ or employed them. The 5th and 7th Circuits are the other appellate courts that have blocked such lawsuits.

But how the Supreme Court will handle this case is far from clear.

This won’t be the first time the Supreme Court has ruled on Title IX employee claims. The high court ruled 5-4 in 2005 that an Alabama high school girls’ basketball coach and gym teacher could proceed with his Title IX lawsuit in Jackson v. Birmingham Board of Education.

Roderick Jackson had complained to supervisors about the unequal funding and access to facilities and equipment his team received but was allegedly ignored. He began receiving negative performance reviews and ultimately lost his job as the girls’ coach though he remained employed as a teacher. He argued officials retaliated against him in violation of Title IX for reporting sex-based discrimination.

To Miriam Becker-Cohen, the Supreme Court’s holding for Jackson should make Crowther a rather easy case. Becker-Cohen is senior appellate counsel at the Constitutional Accountability Center, a nonprofit law firm and think tank, and is the lead author of an amicus brief supporting the plaintiffs.

There is no limitation on who can bring a private lawsuit in any Title IX case, according to Becker-Cohen. “Jackson only makes that case even stronger because Jackson involved a school employee,” she said.

If anything, Becker-Cohen contended that Jackson was a harder case because the court had to resolve the issue of whether it involved intentional sex discrimination. But there is “no question” the acts complained of in Crowther are intentional sex discrimination, she said.

But Jon Israel, a management-side sports and employment law attorney with Foley & Lardner in New York, suggested the cases have differences even though both involve coaches.

The finding in Jackson of an implied right to sue “makes it a little more distinguishable” than the Crowther case, which is dealing with a direct right to sue for nonstudents at higher educational institutions.

Gregory Garre, who served as U.S. solicitor general during the George W. Bush administration and now practices with Latham & Watkins, agrees there is a distinction. Garre filed an amicus brief in the case on behalf of the National School Attorneys Association supporting the University System of Georgia’s regents board.

“When it comes to Title IX, what they’re asking the court to do here is really dramatically expand the right from its traditional focus, which is on sex discrimination against students, into the realm of employment discrimination against employees of schools,” Garre told Higher Ed Dive.

Garre added that Title VII of the Civil Rights Act, which Congress amended shortly before passing Title IX, applies to employees of educational institutions as well as other organizations, meaning they can sue over sex-based employment discrimination under that statute. And, he called that timing significant.

“The statutory history here is a pretty compelling indication that Congress did not intend for Title IX to cover employment discrimination because Congress had passed Title VII [amendments] just months before it enacted Title IX.”

As a result, both Garre and Israel have said they think Title VII provides ample recourse for employees, such as those in the Crowther case.

But Becker-Cohen disagreed. “At least for some employees of federally funded institutions, Title VII isn’t going to be an option,” she said.

While the remedies may overlap, Becker-Cohen said Title VII has a much tighter statute of limitations, which can be as short as 180 days, than Title IX, which has no set limitations period — so courts rely on the most analogous state law limitations period.

She also said Title VII requires individuals to exhaust other avenues, such as filing a complaint with the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission before they can sue, among other procedural roadblocks not present in Title IX.

And that’s not all, according to sports law professor Barbara Osborne, who has represented colleges and universities on compliance issues.

“Title VII is strictly based on the sex of the employee. So that would not include what the typical Title IX claims are. They are different causes of action,” said Osborne, who teaches at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill.

In the typical Title IX employment case, she explained, the person suffering the negative employment action is not necessarily discriminated against because of their sex but rather for complaining about sex discrimination in the athletics program, which she noted that both Jackson and Joseph — the former Georgia Tech coach — clearly did.

Osborne also pointed out that Title IX regulations contain a section related to employment. “Why was it necessary to create regulations related to employment if there weren’t situations within education that might be different than normal employment situations?”

The Supreme Court could have said Title IX remedies were not available for employment retaliation in Jackson and declined to do so, Osborne and Becker-Cohen both were quick to note.

But more than two decades later, Crowther comes before a much different court, with the author of the Jackson opinion, former Justice Sandra Day O’Connor, and others in the majority, having long since retired or died.

College athletes are not considered employees. But that day could be coming in light of the massive changes affecting college sports in recent years, including the influx of name, image and likeness money.

As a result, how the Supreme Court resolves Crowther could affect college athletes themselves should they someday be deemed employees of the institutions whose teams they play on.

“If Title IX includes employees, the class of potential Title IX plaintiffs is going to be expanded dramatically,” said Garre, adding that the possibility of college athletes being considered employees would “certainly create new wrinkles” in litigation.

One of those wrinkles for college-athletes-as-employees could become a case of “be careful what you wish for” according to Israel, if the Court rules against the plaintiffs.

“If the student-athletes are treated as employees, and Crowther decides that Title VII is the exclusive remedy for employees then they’re in that Title VII world — not Title IX.” Israel said.

He contended that some disparate treatment arguments — that an institution is treating female athletes differently than male athletes playing revenue-producing sports, such as football or basketball — might effectively disappear under Title VII as a result.

According to Israel, that’s because a college gymnast’s work, for instance, differs from that of a football player so they would not be similarly situated under Title VII. “If you put it in a different context, a line worker versus an executive, they wouldn’t have the same compensation,” the sports law attorney said.

“The job description of a student-athlete would be virtually identical no matter what team they participate on, right?” she said. “They’re going to be expected to go to class. They’re going to be expected to go to practice, follow the nutrition program and whatever else. The job description isn’t (to) be able to perform in front of 15,000 fans. That’s not what the job is.”

Becker-Cohen acknowledged that if the Supreme Court were to rule for the system’s regents board, it would leave student-athletes and others with no remedy for sex discrimination unless they could make out a claim under Title VII.

“To the extent that the Court carves out college employees from the reach of Title IX’s private right of action, if the class of individuals who are considered college employees increases, that will just harm more people,” Becker-Cohen said.

Correction: The subtitle in a previous version of this story misstated which month the Supreme Court would hear oral arguments in the case. This has been updated.

Colleges would be on the hook for up to $100,000 in fees for each international student seeking to participate in Optional Practical Training.

The American Institute of Architects (AIA)

#7286. Chief Customer Officer at Xapo Bank

Diversity is at the heart of who we are at Xapo Bank. We’re a fully distributed team of over 160 Xapiens that work remotely from 50+ countries around the world.

Our beginning: A world that enjoys economic freedom and wealth protection,no matter where you live or who is running your country.To achieve that, we search the world for the best people for the job. We work hard, think globally, and inspire each other to learn and grow. We are committed to changing the way things are done.

Although we are headquartered in Gibraltar, this is a full time, 100% remote position Work from anywhere!

Xapo's product suite has grown from a Bitcoin vault into a full member proposition: BTC and USD accounts that pay interest, a global card with Bitcoin cashback, Bitcoin-backed loans, a growing suite of BTC wealth and credit products, and access to stocks, ETFs, and stablecoins, all inside one regulated relationship.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

Shape the FutureImprove lives through cutting-edge technology, work 100% remotely from anywhere in the world.

Great work-life balanceBuild amazing things with a balance of autonomy and collaborative teamwork. Set your own work schedule and make use of a flexible PTO plan when you need to recharge.

Expect ExcellenceCollaborate, learn, and grow with a high-performance team. Learn how you learn best - from books to conferences, you’ll get a yearly budget for your individual learning and development goals.

At Xapo, we prioritize consumer protection and adhere to regulatory requirements by ensuring that all Xapiens are accountable for upholding principles of fair treatment, transparency, and ethical conduct in their interactions with customers and stakeholders.

#7285. Abrupt leadership departures at two private universities

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Bard College announced its next leader amid ongoing scrutiny. Plus, a Colorado president will step down after two decades at her university.

Editor’s note: The Leadership Ledger is a monthly roundup of some of the most noteworthy college leadership changes nationwide.

In a month of tricks and treats, colleges across the U.S. got their share of both.

Two public university systems, in Georgia and Wisconsin, announced new leaders following much-discussed departures earlier this year. Conversely, two private universities saw their president abruptly resign in October, with one providing little insight into what prompted the leadership change.

From Texas to Wisconsin, we’re rounding up some of last month’s biggest leadership changes below.

President: Thomas Fanning Institution: University System of Georgia Coming or going? Coming

The University System of Georgia’s governing board on Oct. 1 confirmed former utility executive Thomas Fanning as its next chancellor. Fanning, the sole finalist for the job, is to take office Jan. 1, replacing Sonny Perdue. This will be the first position in higher education for Fanning, who spent more than 40 years in leadership positions at Southern Co. before retiring in 2023.

USG has a history of hiring leaders from outside of academia. Perdue, who announced his intention to retire in April, had a decades-long political career prior to taking the helm of USG in 2022. During his four-year tenure, he installed 14 presidents across the system’s 25 colleges, and USG’s fall enrollment repeatedly broke records. Perdue’s last day is set for Dec. 31.

President: Walter Iwanenko Institution: Gannon University Coming or going? Going

Walter Iwanenko stepped down as president of Gannon University, effective Oct. 8, the Pennsylvania institution announced the next day.

Gannon is in the process of acquiring nearby Ursuline College, a restructuring that Iwanenko said he committed to seeing the university through. The merger of the two Catholic institutions is expected to be complete by mid-December.

“Now that it is nearly done, I am stepping down to pursue other opportunities,” he said in a statement.

Gannon’s board chose Keith Taylor, who previously led the university for over a decade, to replace Iwanenko starting Oct. 8. It indicated Taylor’s appointment would not be a long-term one, saying he would assist the university “through this transition and the search for a new permanent president.”

Shortly after he took office, Taylor said Gannon is preparing for a reduction in force amid a sharp decline in enrollment and a broader institutional restructuring, Erie News Now reported.

President: Daniel Pullin Institution: Texas Christian University Coming or going? Going

Texas Christian University Chancellor Daniel Pullin abruptly resigned on Oct. 22 after less than two years in the role. The announcement came via a campus email sent by Victor Boschini, who headed TCU for over two decades before departing in 2025. Boschini is once again leading TCU after its board appointed him to replace Pullin.

The email did not give a reason for Pullin’s departure.

President: Amy Parsons Institution: Colorado State University Coming or going? Going

Amy Parsons will step down as president of Colorado State University, she said in an Oct. 28 campus letter. Her last day will be Dec. 31.

Parsons, an alumna of CSU, became the public institution's president in 2023 after holding senior leadership positions within the CSU system for nearly two decades.

In an Oct. 28 statement, CSU System Chancellor Tony Frank touted the university’s improved national visibility and the student access initiatives established during Parsons’ tenure. He said the search for her successor will begin in the spring and be led by Rico Munn, the incoming CSU system chancellor who is set to start in mid-2027. Longtime faculty member Rick Miranda, who served as CSU’s interim president in 2022 prior to Parsons' hiring, will again be the university’s acting leader beginning in January.

Like many public institutions, CSU is in a tough place financially amid underwhelming state support. Colorado’s fiscal 2027 budget kept higher education funding flat while capping public colleges’ ability to raise tuition for in-state students at 3.5%. CSU is set to cut some $39 million from its budget next year through staff layoffs and other measures.

President: Marvin Krislov Institution: Bard College Coming or going? Coming

Marvin Krislov will be the next president of Bard College, the private New York institution’s board said on Oct. 29. Krislov currently leads Pace University and previously held the presidency at Oberlin College. Following a unanimous vote, Bard’s board signed Krislov to a three-year contract which will begin July 1.

The announcement comes as Bard continues to navigate leadership challenges and unwanted public attention.

Leon Botstein resigned as Bard president this summer over intense scrutiny focused on his professional relationship with Jeffrey Epstein and Epstein’s resulting involvement with Bard. Botstein had led the campus for five decades.

Jonathan Becker, a longtime Bard senior administrator, has served as acting president in the interim.

Initially, Botstein continued to hold positions at Bard, including a professorship. But he stepped down from those roles this month after Democratic lawmakers made fresh allegations that he may have directly participated in Epstein’s abuse and exploitation of his victims. Botstein denies those allegations.

President: Tonjanita Johnson Institution: Universities of Wisconsin Coming or going? Coming

Tonjanita Johnson will be the next president of the Universities of Wisconsin, following a unanimous vote from the system’s board on Oct. 30. Johnson, a senior administrator with the University of Alabama system, will take office at the start of the new year.

Johnson will be the system’s first permanent leader since trustees ousted its last president, Jay Rothman, in April.

Rothman had said previously that the board had given him an ultimatum — resign or retire by the end of the calendar year, or face termination by the board. He said he declined to step down because the board did not give him a reason for the request. The president of the board disputed this account, saying at the time that Rothman “was not without notice, nor was this process sudden.”

Beginning in 2028, community college districts will be able to create between two and 12 new bachelor's programs, depending on their student outcomes.

Analysts pointed to worse-than-expected fiscal 2026 financials and a projected budget shortfall for the current fiscal year.
